<!--
Hi everyone! I’m Josh – I’m a full time independent open source maintainer. I focus on static analysis tooling around JavaScript & TypeScript – most notably, typescript-eslint, the tooling that allows you to run ESLint and Prettier on TypeScript code. 
I’m also the author of the recent Learning TypeScript book, published by O’Reilly.
-->

<!--
This is the technical, precise term for TypeScript: that it’s a “superset” of JavaScript. Meaning, it’s a programming language that contains all the syntax of JavaScript, plus new syntax.
-->

<!--
This variable, ‘value’, is given an initial value of type string. That means value is only ever allowed to be a string. If TypeScript sees that we try to provide it a number, it’ll yell at us in the form of a type error.
Note that this code is syntactically valid – so it can be transpiled down to output JavaScript. But TypeScript will log an error when doing so.
But, what if we have a variable whose type can’t be inferred rom the initial value? In that case, we’ll need some way of indicating to TypeScript what the intended type is meant to be.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play?#code/DYUwLgBAbghsCuIIF4ICICSAVA6gZTQG4AoYgSwDMIAKAWRjAAsA6AJxgDsATAewFtqASggA+CAAZmAVmEBvYhEXQ4iFOlbxQALyLEAvhBDAAzknlLlCJKgCMAZjsB2EhYD0riFgCeAByQByDng+ACMQVn8IMmMIDh5IGGNjMgBzDhgQ0AgwHmzfAOMwVjIOFP9mfWIgA
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 350 }
---

<!--
A union type represents a value that can be any of multiple possible types. Which, unlike many traditional languages, is totally allowed in JavaScript/TypeScript!
This ‘value’ variable can be either a string or a number. It’s entirely random which one it is. Which means its type is ‘string | number’ (pronounced “string or number”).
For union typed values, TypeScript only allows accessing properties that exist on all ”constituent types” in that union. Here, that’s properties that exist on both string and number. `toString()` works here and in many unions.
But, properties that exist on only one of the constituent types aren’t allowed. They might not be there at runtime! Here, `.toUpperCase()` exists on number but not string, while `.toPrecision()` exists on string but not number.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: view-transition, duration: 500 }
---

<!--
Enter type annotations: a way to indicate to TypeScript the type of a construct such as a variable.
Variables that are given a type annotation also stay the same type always. Here we’re saying that value is type string, and so are not allowed to provide a number (or any other not-string typed values).
Keep in mind that type annotations are purely a development-time construct. They’re erased when your TypeScript syntax is transpiled over to JavaScript.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play?#code/DYUwLgBAbghsCuIBcEDOYBOBLAdgcwG4AoIrAMwgAoBZGMACwDoMYcATAewFtKBKCAHwQADIwCs-AN5EIs6HEQQAvBABEwOMACeq4gF8IIYKhARpc+QlMqAjAGY7AdmIWA9K4gAVLQAdTAchx4LgAjEAx-CCxUCBwOSBhUVCw8HBgQ0AgwDizfAPRsfH9GIj0iIA
-->

These type annotations are the first bits of TypeScript-specific syntax we’ve seen in this talk. They’re not a part of JavaScript. When you “transpile” your code from TypeScript to JavaScript, they’re erased.
-->

<!--
You can describe union types in type annotations just as you would other types, such as primitives. That means you can indicate to TypeScript that a variable is going to eventually be a union type. Or, in other words, you’re telling TypeScript that the value variable is allowed to be either a string or a number.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play?#code/DYUwLgBAbghsCuIBcEDOYBOBLAdgcwgB8Id4BbAIxAwG4AoOrAMwgAoBZGMACwDoMYOACYB7MqwCUEAHwQADLwCsUgN50IG6HEQQAvBABEABRA4AxlmAH6AXwghgqEBDWatCZ-oCMAZh8B2WwZYDz0ITER6AHooiAAVAE8AB2cAcgoREVBBVIgsVBIRSBhUVCw8HBgKUHCRcOS09Gx8IhJyKgxU3jogA
-->

<!--
Union types enable a concept called “narrowing” in TypeScript. Narrowing is when TypeScript understands that inside some area of code, a value must be a more narrow type than it was thought to be. This happens when a block of code can only be reached when a value is a particular type.
Here, the if statement’s block is only reachable if the value is a string. TypeScript has deduced that the type of value inside that block is string, not number | string.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play?#code/MYewdgzgLgBAbgQwDYFcCmMC8MCyCoAWAdAE4JgAmIAtgBQCUMAfDAAxECsMA-DAEQApEBAJ8YALhgBGAMwyA7AG4AUMsSo0RKCACqABz1oSAYQQQ0DFcoD01mAAUSIQySgBPGAHJt+l6fOeMFRoEDBgILBoAB4AltAw4DDuhl6CwqIwAD7ScvKeRMoxAGYwtMloICXq6FiY2HzQJDFgAOZ8jADeyjA98MjoWroGRv4W9CoAvspAA
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 350 }
---

<!--
This brings us to the concept of literal types. A literal type represents a single instance of a primitive. They’re more narrow than primitives: we don’t mean any instance of that type, we mean a single particular instance.
Here, the value string is only allowed to be the string `”RPI”`. IT’s not allowed to be something else, such as `”RIT”` or `string | null`.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play?#code/DYUwLgBAbghsCuIBcEBEAlACgSVQbgCgDYEQIBeNLXQ4uRCq7AFXwIHp2JmBPABzIByDC1SCIASwDOEAHYB7SDClSJAc1kwARqAhh5e-kIw4xAOiIkGlPgCd5AWz5gAFKgDuACxhhAmAQyYCDV5eQATCAAreQBrMm15eEhqAH5UAEpCTm4jCEEpMFsJWTUIAB85eGBgcWk5RQhlVQ1tXX1DAVyTXF8LIA
-->

<!--
Literal types can be mix-and-matched with primitives. This union here indicates a value that can be any string in the world or the literal -1. Other numbers, such as 1337, can’t be assigned to it.

One common place literals show up in real world code is with nullability: values that can be something or undefined, alternately something or null. These “nullable” unions represent some value that is either given a primitive, or can be undefined.

<!--
JavaScript code tends to have a lot of objects. TypeScript would be pretty useless if it didn’t understand them.
When you create a variable with an object value, TypeScript understands that variable to be of an object type. That object type contains all the same properties as the variable, with the primitive (not literal!) type equivalents for all its values.
 
Here, the ‘value’ variable is an object with a ‘name’ string and a ‘power’ number. Accessing those two properties is allowed, and TypeScript knows what type they’ll be. But accessing a ‘nope’ property is not allowed – TypeScript knows it doesn’t exist!
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play?#code/MYewdgzgLgBAbgQwDYFcCmMC8MDeMwIC2aAXDAEQBSIEAFuQDQwAOIA7mgE5kCcADHwCMMAL4BuAFATEqNADoCxSTPRzWHTnKggACpzTAAlhEPgAFAEpJ05KrAhmaSQHpnMPQ65QAnjADk9o5+MAAmIGgQ+CCwaAAexrDgMD6OEq7+eIqkMNCchmAA5mIs7FxkYCiEAEZcxSJ+chJAA
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 350 }

</span><span style="color:#9CDCFE;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">power</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">: </span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">number</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px"> &#125;;</span></p><p style="font-size:18px;line-height:19.44px;margin-top:10px"><br><span style="font-size:18px;text-decoration:line-through">value</span><span style="color:#9CDCFE;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px"> = &#123; </span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">name: </span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">"John"</span><span style="color:#CE9178;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">, </span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">power: </span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">9001</span><span style="color:#B5CEA8;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px"> &#125;;</span></p><p style="font-size:18px;line-height:19.44px;margin-top:10px"><br><span style="font-size:18px;text-decoration:line-through">value</span><span style="color:#9CDCFE;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px"> = &#123; <!--
Object types can be placed in type annotations, too. They look like regular JavaScript objects, but have types instead of values, and semicolons instead of commas.
When you declare a variable to be an object type, TypeScript will make sure any value assigned to that variable structurally matches that object type. All required properties declared on the object type must exist in that object value, and they must have the same type.
Here, we’re declaring a ‘value’ variable to be an object with a ‘name’ string and a ‘power’ number. We can assign an object value with ‘name’ string and ‘power number’. But if we forget a property, or use the wrong type of value for a property, then TypeScript will yell at us.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play?#code/DYUwLgBAbghsCuIBcEDeEB2MC2yIGcwAnASwwHMBuCABwHsB3EIlDebAI2YgF9KAofrAQgIAXjSYceAEQApOgAsMMgDS1GzFAE4ADLoCMvAULiJxkrLhQyAwiCwzj-APQuIABSJ0azMAE8IAHJ6JiIgiBJ8CGwo-DJySIwIAN9g9Cs8QlIKah4g13cOeEgiEABHeBIygBMklP80oIzpFGyE6lCtTHYuIjyggDpBYXMJFusIeAwakAAzMhAa9S6WCD1DZzcIABVG0SDp2YWMJYiozDpIGHx48iwOUBS6Bqb2iiH+IA
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 350 }

</span><span style="color:#9CDCFE;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">name</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">: </span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">string</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">; </span><span style="color:#9CDCFE;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">power</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">: </span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">number</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px"> &#125;;</span></p><p style="font-size:18px;line-height:19.44px;margin-top:10px"><br><span style="font-size:18px;text-decoration:line-through">let </span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">value</span><span style="color:#9CDCFE;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">: </span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">NameAndPower</span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px">;</span></p><p style="font-size:18px;line-height:19.44px;margin-top:10px"><br><span style="font-size:18px;text-decoration:line-through">value</span><span style="color:#9CDCFE;font-family:'Menlo';font-size:18px"> = &#123; <!--
It can be useful to assign a name to an object shape, such as when you want to reuse it multiple times, or get an error message that doesn’t have a bunch of lines of syntax in it. TypeScript provides a ‘type’ keyword to create an alias for the type. Think of it as a “copy and paste” keyword: you provide a name and a type, and from that point on, TypeScript knows that whenever you type the name, what you really meant was that type.
This code snippet is almost the same as the previous slide’s. The ‘value’ variable is a type that has a ‘name’ string and a ‘power’ number. The only difference is now, we’re calling that type ‘NameAndPower’.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play?#code/C4TwDgpgBAcghgWwgQQHYBMAKB7A7hAJygF4oBvKVRCALigGdgCBLVAcwG4ow9C7UArggBGhKAF8OAKCkAbCMCgA3OLIG1Y1NFl4FpUlWuikKVJHQBEAKWwALVBYA03XXQCcABg8BGCfsPqJOSU1JYAwhBUFn5SAPSxUJgE2JAEoFAA5Dz4BBlQzPRQCAX0rGz5qFCgkJmmoQxMZVziGXEJwgKKBBAAjgLM3egVVeDQGfBI2jg5GQB0MgHGwWYaAhgQAGasEOjO2XxQnj4x8VAAKqOZa+ib2+h5BZTYinD0pWxUwvJV2CM1GYwWOw5jIgA
I’ll also note that TypeScript has a syntax feature called “interfaces” that are often used for this purpose (reusable object shapes). They’re almost exactly the same, and the differences aren’t something we need to cover today. Don’t worry about it until you thoroughly learn TypeScript.
-->

<!--
This brings us to my favorite feature in TypeScript: discriminated type unions. They’re an awesome combination of two key portions of the type system we’ve covered: object types and union types. And in most real world code, we get literal types thrown in as well.
A discriminated type union is one where one member (the “discriminant”) exists with the same name but different value in each constituent type. Checking the value of the discriminant acts as a form of type narrowing. In other words, a discriminated type union is a union of object types where one member’s value indicates which of those object types the value is.
This slide’s NameOrPower type is a union of two object types: NameValue and PowerValue. Its ‘value’ variable is always going to be one of those two types. The only property in common between them is ‘variant’: which can be either “name” or “power”. But, if we narrow down that ‘variant’ is “name”, then TypeScript knows that the value must be the NameValue type! So it can type narrow inside the if block and the else block.
https://www.typescriptlang.org/play?#code/C4TwDgpgBAcghgWwgNTgGwK7QLxQN5QB2iEAXFAM7ABOAloQOYDcUok5ARMUh1AL5MAUG2gAFAPYB3CNVSYc+KGCkzyhDAgBGMliM7Lp1Xn0HDw0eEgDy1CYai5LKdFigAfKHZlysQwQGNxQiooADcXMlgSGy9qBygAWThgAAsAOmo4QgATcQQACgBKKAA+KAAGNIBWQSg6qAB+RW5IjgApcQoUjgAaVnNOFuNa+vICA1UoAE5y8oBGPr0oDgmjfj9w+RYAem3FJa4SXg8VlTWTQVoAMyh8zaw0kQdsXEOeYrwRuvuIHb2nHwQQR8KAQNAUaCfephCJ-TxnQHAwRAA
-->
